#2822. New car prices are at a record high. Here's where to look for deals

Consumers who are in the market for a new car may find the latest pricing data somewhat sobering.

The average transaction price for a new vehicle reached an all-time high of $51,109 in October, up 2.1% from a year earlier, according to a report from Kelley Blue Book, an auto valuation company. The amount excludes incentives, taxes and fees.

But for consumers who can't spend that much — or don't want to — the good news is that plenty of buyers are shelling out much less for a vehicle.

"The average is telling us as much about product mix as it is about affordability," Erin Keating, executive analyst at Cox Automotive, which owns Kelley Blue Book, said in the report.

Of the five highest-volume car types sold in October, three — compact and subcompact SUVs, and compact cars — sold at prices far below the overall average transaction price, she said. For example, the average amount paid for a compact car in October was $28,030.

Additionally, sales of vehicles priced from about $28,000 to $38,000 climbed 9.6% from a year earlier.

"Consumers are still buying plenty of vehicles well below the headline average transaction price, even as strong demand for full-size pickups and other larger, well-equipped vehicles keeps pushing the average to new highs," Keating said.

The record price comes amid a 34% jump in the average cost of a new vehicle since October 2019, when it was $38,259, according to Kelley Blue Book data.

While pandemic-related supply shortages helped drive up prices earlier this decade, consumers' appetite for larger, more expensive vehicles like SUVs and pickup trucks has also pushed the average higher. Full-size pickups, for instance, sold for an average of $67,912 in October, according to Kelley Blue Book.

Yet buyers who would struggle to afford a $50,000-plus new car can still spend less and find deals in the current market.

For starters, automakers have been slower than usual to roll out 2027 models, at least in part because 2026 cars continue to sell well at current prices, said Ivan Drury, director of insights at Edmunds, an automotive research company. In October, 76% of new-vehicle sales were for current-year models, compared with 59% a year earlier.

This slowdown in the annual model-year transition means more 2027 cars will hit dealer lots soon, which suggests dealers and automakers are motivated to clear 2026 inventory, Drury said.

But waiting until the last week of the year to buy — thinking dealerships are desperate to make the sale — won't necessarily get you a better deal, he said.

"You're not going to get massive savings by waiting," Drury said. "Everybody's busiest time of the year is between Christmas and New Year's, but it's not like the best deals are then — that's a perception thing."

Some manufacturers are offering incentives on certain 2026 models, which can reduce the cost of a new car by thousands of dollars.

For example, the 2026 Nissan Pathfinder, a midsize SUV with a starting price of $41,565 — which includes destination charges — comes with a rebate of $5,000 for qualified buyers through Nov. 30, according to Cars.com, an online automotive marketplace.

Just be sure to check your local dealerships to confirm participation for any manufacturer's incentives, experts say.

Look for cars that have been sitting on dealer lots for an extended time, Drury said. In September, new cars took 58 days to sell, on average, according to Edmunds.

Generally, dealerships consider cars unsold for 60 to 90 days to be aging inventory, which may motivate them to offer a better deal, according to Autotrader.com, an online auto marketplace owned by Cox Automotive.

"You'll find the vehicles that dealers definitely want to get rid of the most," Drury said. Looking for older inventory will be "your quickest way to find a dealership that's thinking, 'Yes, I too want to get rid of this. Help me help you,'" he said.

Listings on some car-shopping websites include how long a car has been on a dealership's lot. You can also ask the dealership how long a particular car has been in its inventory.

Leasing may present an opportunity to get into a car with lower monthly payments than financing the same vehicle, depending on the terms of the lease.

However, fewer attractive leasing deals are available, a trend that started during the pandemic, Drury said. Plus, leases, which are typically for three years, usually come with mileage limits and charges for excessive wear and tear.

Nevertheless, leasing may make financial sense for a consumer who "gets that two- or three-year itch for a new car," Drury said.

This is because after the first two or three years of making payments on a car loan — a time period when the steepest depreciation generally occurs — the car's value may be less than what is still owed on the loan, depending on the specific terms. This is known as being upside down or underwater, or having negative equity. In that case, the amount must either be paid off or added to the buyer's new loan.

"We see so many cars traded in that are 3 years old, and the customer is upside down by $7,000, $8,000 or $10,000 and they roll that into another loan," Drury said.

You also may be able to find a good deal on a used car that's not too old.

The average transaction price for a 3-year-old vehicle was $32,039 in the third quarter, down slightly from $32,461 in the second quarter but 3.1% higher than $31,067 a year earlier, according to Edmunds. It's also higher than the average $27,133 list price for used cars of any age in October, according to Cox.

However, these 3-year-old cars are selling for far less than they did when they were new — $16,562 below the original sticker price of $48,601, on average. That marks the largest dollar gap Edmunds has on record, surpassing the previous high of $16,252 in the last quarter of 2019.

Some 3-year-old models tend to take longer to sell than others, which may give buyers some negotiating power, according to Edmunds. For example, a 3-year-old Jeep Cherokee, with an average transaction price of $23,138 — compared with its original price of $42,263 — takes an average of 64 days to sell, according to Edmunds.

Additionally, a good credit score can help keep the cost of financing a car purchase down. The higher your score, the lower the interest rate you can get, generally speaking.

And, finally, if you're trading in a car, be sure to shop it around, Drury said.

"Get multiple offers. They can vary by thousands of dollars," Drury said. "Have those offers in hand when you're at the dealership that has the next car you want to buy."

#2819. Luka Dončić says he asked Lakers front office to acquire Walker Kessler: "I went to them and said, 'I want Walker"'

One of the Los Angeles Lakers' biggest offseason moves is paying off big so far — and Luka Dončić is taking a bit of credit for making it happen.

Lakers center Walker Kessler, who was acquired from the Utah Jazz in a sign-and-trade deal in July, has been a key piece of the team on both ends of the court so far this season. And as it turns out, Dončić told ESPN's Dave McMenamin that he played a role in Kessler's arrival in L.A.

In McMenamin's profile on Kessler published Friday, Dončić said that he spoke to Rob Pelinka, the Lakers' president of basketball operations and general manager, during the offseason about acquiring the big man from the Jazz. Dončić said that he was inspired by Kessler's impressive defense at the rim after playing against him in previous seasons.

"I told them after the season I wanted Walker," Dončić told ESPN, speaking about that offseason conversation. "I think he's a really good player … Obviously, last season he didn't play. He played like five games. Obviously [Utah] kept him out, but just playing against him in the previous years, it was hard to score. He was protecting the rim, he was dominating the paint. So just after the season, I went to them and said, 'I want Walker.'"

Dončić credits his burgeoning chemistry with Kessler as a key piece of the team's growth. When speaking about his pick-and-roll with Kessler, Dončić told ESPN: "When we get good at that … we can be really good."

In response, Kessler told ESPN that he was honored to have Dončić want him as a teammate.

"To have someone like that, with that pedigree, to say, 'Hey, I want that to be our center'... it was incredible," Kessler said. "I was like, 'Wow, this dude, he believes in me. He knows that I can help this team.' It makes you excited to play."

In many ways, the Kessler trade was risky: The Lakers sent two unprotected first-round picks (2031 and 2033) and two first-round swaps (2028 and 2030) for the 25-year-old big man, who only played in five games last year after undergoing season-ending shoulder surgery in November 2025.

But so far, the move has paid off. Kessler is averaging team-leading 10.8 rebounds and 2.1 blocks per game, and is the Lakers' third-highest scorer with 14 points per game. His 66.9% field-goal percentage is one of the best in the NBA.

It's not a huge surprise that Dončić, who is the Lakers' last remaining superstar after the offseason departure of LeBron James, has filled in the role of player-GM. Dončić is the face of the team and the leading scorer, and seems to have gained significant influence in the Lakers front office.

After the Kessler trade went through in July, Dončić said at the time that he was "excited" about the addition. Reports at the time indicated that the team was following Dončić's two biggest requests, which were to retain Austin Reaves (who signed a four-year, $185 million extension this summer) and bring in a rim protector like Kessler.

The trio of Dončić, Reaves and Kessler have led the Lakers to a 15-3 record to start the season, putting them at the top of the Western Conference.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2818. AI debt markets shudder as Nvidia's top 'banker' returns

AI credit markets are shuddering again with Jensen Huang back as CEO of Nvidia.

Fears about the massive debt being taken on to fund the artificial intelligence buildout climbed Tuesday as Wall Street weighed what Huang's return to Nvidia means for its bankrolling of the AI boom.

Huang is considered one of the top financiers, or bankers, in the AI industry, spearheading deals with neoclouds like CoreWeave and other firms to buy chips, backed in some cases by Nvidia guarantees. His return to Nvidia has revived prospects for the company to continue "circular financing" of AI chips and data centers — raising concerns among credit investors.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child of AI debt concerns, saw its stock sink 13% and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 top 17%. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

At the same time, the spread on the neocloud company's five-year credit default swaps jumped 8.5% to over 1,200 basis points. Credit default swaps are insurance against nonpayment and are considered a barometer of perceived risks. Rising CDS spreads mean investors view the debt as riskier.

Huang was reinstated as CEO on Monday after stepping down on Nov. 12 amid allegations of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. His reinstatement on Monday was cheered by investors, who pushed stocks higher, yields lower and spreads on CDS tighter. However, stocks retreated, bond yields rose and CDS spreads widened on Tuesday.

Ben Emons, founder and chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the "Jensen effect."

The moves suggest "markets see government intervention as 'moral suasion' that carries credit and interest-rate risks," he said.

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday he "convinced" the board to bring Huang back.

CoreWeave wasn't the only name seeing heightened concerns in CDS spreads. Oracle's five-year CDS spread jumped 7% to 355.68. Even highly rated hyperscalers Meta Platforms and Amazon.com saw spreads widen by more than 2%. Nvidia's CDS added 3.4%.

Larry Holzenthaler, senior portfolio manager for fixed income at Catalyst Funds, believes the drama that unfolded with Huang's return to Nvidia won't necessarily sit well with credit investors.

"A lot of old school credit people are going to look at what kind of materialized there, the administration's involvement in putting him back in, and I think a lot of credit participants are going to step back and tiptoe away from that a bit," he said. "Credit market investors typically don't like that type of drama or uncertainty."

While Nvidia has commitments across the AI ecosystem, it is the neoclouds that have been under the most scrutiny. Many of the companies, which rent out AI computing power and cloud capacity, are highly leveraged and have relied on Nvidia financing to reduce their exposure to the bond market.

With so much riding on Nvidia's funding, Huang's next move is being closely watched.

During his brief absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress said during the company's earnings call that Nvidia would be "rigorously evaluating future investments" and will remain disciplined in how it commits capital.

That suggested Nvidia was going to be more judicious with financing, says Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"That concern should be put to rest with Jensen [Huang] back," he said.

Investor Steve Eisman, of "Big Short" fame and host of "The Real Eisman Playbook" podcast, believes Huang feels emboldened now that he's back in charge.

"[H]e'll double down on the circular financing," Eisman predicted on CNBC's "Squawk Box" Tuesday.

After Huang's departure, Eisman believed that the ecosystem the CEO created by pouring money into AI companies would likely unwind and leave some of the companies in "big trouble."

"Now that he's back, they're not," he said.

Others are not so certain the level of funding will continue.

"Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang's return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing," Stifel analyst Ruben Roy said in a note Monday.

Nvidia said on its earnings call that it has $118 billion in guarantees, as of Oct. 25, to back AI projects.

Concerns about the level of debt to fund the AI infrastructure buildout had already taken hold before Huang's departure and reinstatement.

"These issues have not disappeared just because the White House pressured the weak board to reinstate Jensen Huang," said economist David Rosenberg, founder and president of Rosenberg Research.

John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson Investors, believes having Huang back at the helm "reaffirms his existing go-to-market strategy." However, the credit market is still taking a cautious look at the neoclouds as it waits for more clarity on long-term supply/demand dynamics.

Still, Lloyd is cautiously positive on CoreWeave, which he owns in portfolios he manages, and noted all the data point to a "pretty healthy environment right now." That includes strong residual values on graphics processing units and spot compute rates, he noted.

"The company is doing everything in its power to execute," he said. "They don't need the high yield market at this time."

In the meantime, investigations into Nvidia and Huang are ramping up. Huang is expected to appear before three House committees next week. Nvidia is conducting an independent investigation into his activities, and California Attorney General Rob Bonta has launched a probe. At the federal level, President Trump said Monday that U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"There's other shoes to drop," Rosenberg said. "I don't think that the book has been closed on this file."

#2817. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

#2814. Budget airline Norse Atlantic files for bankruptcy in Europe after failing to secure funding

Budget carrier Norse Atlantic filed for bankruptcy Friday, saying it failed to secure funding after soaring fuel costs strained the company's operations throughout the year.

Its parent company, Norse Atlantic ASA, concluded that the Norwegian carrier no longer has a basis to continue operations.

"Despite extensive efforts, the Company has so far not been able to secure sufficient funding to meet its short-term liquidity needs," the firm said in a statement Friday.

It comes days after Norse said it's facing a "critical financial situation" and was considering its options from a strategic takeover to ACMI contracts, which refer to short-term capacity leasing. The company's shares closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

The board has now filed a bankruptcy petition with the Agder District Court, where a trustee will be appointed to manage the process further.

"The Board of Norse Atlantic Airways AS has made the difficult decision to file for bankruptcy despite extensive efforts from the Company and the Group to find a solution," Norse Atlantic CEO Eivind Roald said.

"We are deeply sorry for the impact this has on our passengers, employees and partners. The Board, management and advisers of the listed entity Norse Atlantic ASA and the UK subsidiary are working to find a solution for the remaining part of the business to continue operations," Roald added.

The company has canceled all flights, and ticket sales have been discontinued.

Norse was founded in March 2021 and offered cheap flights between Europe, North America, Africa and Asia.

The Iran war began on Feb. 28 and caused jet fuel prices to skyrocket, forcing airlines around the world to grapple with higher costs. Prices are now up nearly 72% to $155 per barrel year-on-year as of the week ending Nov. 20, according to the International Air Transport Association's Jet Fuel Price Monitor.

Norse Atlantic's Roald told CNBC in May that he wasn't "overly concerned" about access to fuel but flagged that the company had canceled over 700 flights, which impacted more than 240,000 passengers.

Ryanair's CEO Michael O'Leary warned in April that if jet fuel prices continued to stay elevated throughout the year, then other European airlines were likely to face "financial difficulties" during the winter season.

In fact, several European airlines have stopped operating this winter, including Slovakia's AirExplore, Spain's AlbaStar, Ireland's Fly4 and Spain's Plus Ultra, which has canceled all flights but hasn't permanently closed.

#2813. Africa’s richest man is taking his mega-refinery public in a blockbuster IPO

One of the world's largest oil refineries is set to become Africa's biggest public firm in a highly anticipated offering that the continent's richest man has marketed as an "IPO for the people."

Dangote Petroleum Refinery and Petrochemicals FZE, founded by Nigerian billionaire Aliko Dangote, is expected to begin trading in Lagos on Dec. 1, according to multiple media reports, following regulatory approval. A Dangote Refinery spokesperson did not respond to a CNBC request for comment.

Reuters' calculations put Dangote Refinery's IPO-implied valuation at about $47 billion, based on the offer price and the company's registered share count. That would make it the biggest stock market listing Africa has ever seen.

The Lagos-based refinery is regarded as a crown jewel of Nigeria's energy industry given the facility's role in transforming the oil-rich country's fuel market since it started operating in 2024.

"With a minimum subscription of just 10 shares, the offer is designed to give ordinary Nigerians and Africans an opportunity to own a stake in the refinery," Aliko Dangote said at the IPO launch in early September.

"The IPO is positioned not simply as a capital-raising exercise, but as an opportunity for workers, entrepreneurs and everyday Nigerians to participate in the refinery's future growth," he added.

Built for around $20 billion, the Dangote Refinery is now one of the largest in the world, with a crude oil distillation capacity of between 650,000 to 700,000 barrels per day.

It has emerged as a key provider of jet fuel in recent months, with buyers pivoting to West Africa amid Middle Eastern supply disruptions due to the Iran war.

"In theory, it's a win-win situation," Cyril Widdershoven, a maritime, energy and geopolitical analyst at Blue Water Strategy, told CNBC by phone.

Dangote Refinery has bolstered its reputation as a key Atlantic Basin facility amid soaring global demand for refined petroleum products, Widdershoven said, and the company intends to double its refining capacity to 1.4 million barrels per day by 2029, which could further strengthen its competitiveness in global markets.

However, despite the refinery's importance to Nigeria's economy, questions remain over its investment case, Widdershoven said, citing construction costs, commissioning delays, and the possibility that new capacity comes online with weaker margins.

"Right now it's like Anthropic or Nvidia, you are now buying the hype," Widdershoven said.

The Dangote Refinery has had "an extremely strong year," Mick Strautmann, market analyst at Vortexa, told CNBC by phone.

Between January and October, Vortexa data showed Dangote's seaborne refined product loadings stood at around 400,000 barrels per day, which is roughly 50% more than the same period last year.

More recently, however, the facility has seen refined-product loading jump to a record of 575,000 barrels per day through the first three weeks of November, surpassing an October record of 550,000 barrels per day, Strautmann said.

"It's been able to ramp up quite spectacularly and provide a lot of these products to the market in an extremely high price environment," Strautmann added.

"So, it's been one of the few places that's adding a lot of fuel supply to the Atlantic Basin, and at the same time, you know, to make West Africa less reliant on imports from other regions, particularly Europe for its gasoline supply," he said.

David Omojomolo, an emerging market economist focused on Africa at Capital Economics, said the upcoming Dangote Refinery IPO has captured global attention because the facility is seen as a rare example of an industrial policy success story on the African continent.

"It's been a good news story, and it's been an optimistic one as well," Omojomolo told CNBC.

"I don't want to be a hostage to fortune and say this time is different … but it does feel like a buzz and it's a question as to does it force other, say, billionaire industrialists to put their money where their mouth is in terms of helping the region's industrialisation prospects," he added.

#2812. Nvidia's billion-dollar board bet: Who's protected as lawsuits mount?

Jensen Huang is back as CEO of Nvidia, after President Donald Trump urged the company's board to rehire Huang. But investigations into the company, its executive leadership and its board continue as outside criticism mounts.

"Clearly they caved to external pressure," said Betsy Atkins, an author and longtime public-company director. "The board failed," she said, when it initially allowed Huang's removal. "The board's role is to think of all stakeholders."

California Attorney General Rob Bonta is investigating whether Nvidia misrepresented information to investors. "There is classic corporate securities law on the books in California that prevents investors and the public from being misled by companies," Bonta said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Tuesday.

Directors are already defendants in a shareholder lawsuit accusing them of failing to oversee Nvidia. A separate securities class action targets the company and Huang, its co-founder.

Now the board’s insurance choices are part of the story. A risk advisor with direct knowledge of Nvidia’s coverage, who asked not to be named because they are not authorized to speak publicly about client matters, said the company built an approximately $1 billion D&O tower, insurance jargon for directors and officers insurance policies from different companies that combine to cover one massive risk.

Nvidia "loves insurance," the advisor said. “They buy insurance as if they have no balance sheet.”

A billion-dollar tower would essentially mean Nvidia "bought the market," purchasing all the coverage it could get, according to another industry expert, who has 25 years working in financial insurance. The expert asked not to be named due to sensitivity surrounding relationships with past or potential future clients.

But Nvidia directed all that coverage to Side A only, according to the risk advisor. Side A protects directors and officers personally when the company cannot indemnify them. Side B covers the company for legal defense costs or settlements on behalf of directors and officers, and Side C protects the company when it's named as a defendant in a suit such as a securities claim.

The risk advisor says Nvidia believed it had "the war chest" to protect itself against a securities class action suit.

That choice is raising questions about whether the board protected itself at the company’s expense. CNBC asked Nvidia for comment but received no response.

An insurance CEO, who asked not to be named in order to protect client relationships, characterized a billion-dollar policy as a lot of coverage, but said a Side A-only allocation was a surprising move because it results in imbalanced coverage, protecting only directors.

That structure is not automatically a governance failure, according to the D&O insurance expert with long experience in coverage for banks, drugmakers and other businesses that often face litigation. Side A-only programs are common among companies with the capital to manage the corporate risk internally.

"For a company with Nvidia’s resources, retaining the company-side risk can make financial sense," said the industry expert with 25 years working in financial insurance. It's Side A D&O that's "imperative to corporate governance."

"It is a capital-allocation decision," the expert said. The company decides that buying insurance for its own balance sheet is not worth the premium. In other words, Side B and Side C are considered too expensive for the coverage the company gets.

The question for shareholders is whether the board’s decision to hold that risk was a sound use of the company’s resources and whether the company can absorb the costs if the claims grow.

A shareholder derivative complaint filed Nov. 11 names Huang, directors and other officers. It alleges that they failed to oversee risks involving Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang and made misleading statements. It also alleges that some defendants sold roughly $2 billion in Nvidia stock while holding material nonpublic information. Those remain allegations, not findings.

A separate securities class action, filed Nov. 9, names Nvidia and Huang. It seeks damages for investors who bought Nvidia shares between Oct. 9, 2025, and Nov. 8, 2026, alleging the company and Huang misled investors.

The distinction matters: The derivative suit is brought on Nvidia’s behalf, while the class action seeks recovery for investors.

"Insurance is already at work," said Michel Léonard, chief economist at the Insurance Information Institute, describing standard policy mechanics, not Nvidia’s specific policy. "Once the suit is filed, D&O starts paying defense costs, long before anyone proves anything."

A claim still has to meet the policy’s terms, and defense costs can reduce the remaining limits. Nor does a drop in Nvidia’s market value by itself establish an insurable loss. Plaintiffs would need to prove legally recoverable damages; the policy would respond only to covered losses.

The board has already taken steps it says are aimed at accountability. Nvidia announced an independent investigation on Nov. 8, and independent directors retained outside counsel two days later. Huang resigned as CEO and a director on Nov. 11; the board appointed CFO Colette Kress interim CEO. Nvidia said it had no reason to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised by Huang's extramarital relationships.

Atkins, who joined the board of Wynn Resorts in 2018 after founder Steve Wynn resigned, said allegations are not conclusions and that boards should also weigh governance concerns against the value a founder brings to a company’s innovation. Nvidia's investigation is continuing even as Huang resumes leadership.

“Will companies’ hyper-accelerated innovation speed be the same without them? I don’t think so,” said Atkins, author of "Don't Waste a Board Seat: A Decision-Maker's Guide to Choosing Directors Who Create Value."

Nvidia’s 2026 proxy also says the company has indemnity agreements with its directors and officers — separate from its insurance. Those agreements provide for the company to cover certain costs and liabilities when the law permits.

#2811. Saudi Arabia shifts Vision 2030 spending amid economic and security pressure

Saudi Arabia's economic diversification strategy has moved from "spending and delivery at any cost" to "spending and delivery at the right cost," Economy Minister Faisal Alibrahim told CNBC on Friday.

It comes after the Kingdom scaled back or cut several flagship projects as it endures a sharp economic contraction.

Alibrahim also said Saudi Arabia would "stop at nothing from realizing long-term sustainable stability in the region" amid ongoing Houthi strikes on civilian and energy infrastructure that have killed and injured dozens and repeatedly rocked oil markets.

"The phase we're in today is one where every riyal is deployed with greater precision... it was clear that while we want to diversify or restructure our economy to depend less on government spend, there was a lot of catch-up that needed to be done, and for that, you saw government spend leading the way," Alibrahim told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

"Right now, that is shifting from spending and delivery at any cost to spending and delivering at the right cost. We're moving towards a policy-first approach to many of our policy areas and... objectives. We're also creating a clearer business environment, one that aligns the regulatory environment in the Kingdom with international laws."

That includes the recent announcement of a 15% minimum effective tax rate on multinational profits, which Alibrahim said would "create a clearer environment for the private sector to operate," as the government looks to attract more external investment.

Saudi Arabia has been on a mammoth capital spending spree since it launched its Vision 2030 plan to reduce its dependence on oil revenue in 2016, deploying the resources of its sovereign wealth fund — the Public Investment Fund, or PIF.

But more recently, it has reassessed the cost and commercial prospects of initiatives such as the new economic region NEOM and the futuristic "The Line" city, withdrawn funding from the LIV Golf tournament and halted construction on a planned giant skyscraper in Riyadh. In January, it said it would postpone hosting the 2029 Asian Winter Games, which had been scheduled for Trojena, a planned part of NEOM.

Saudi Arabia is meanwhile grappling with the destabilizing effects of ongoing strikes by Iran-backed Houthi militants in Yemen, which have hit targets including the PIF tower, Riyadh Airport and the country's largest oil refinery. The danger forced the Kingdom to call off its big international investor conference, dubbed "Davos in the Desert," which was due to be attended by some of the biggest figures in global finance in November.

"The human cost of crisis has been real, and so has been the economic cost," Alibrahim told CNBC.

Alibrahim said infrastructure investments had allowed the Kingdom's energy sector to restore production to near pre-crisis levels. It has meanwhile moved its import and export hub from the east to the west of the country via land-based transport due to the airport strikes, which has restored cargo levels.

He added that the attacks highlight the importance of diversification to the Saudi economy.

Real GDP contracted by 3.8% in the third quarter, according to government figures, with oil activities down 20.4% year-on-year partially offset by a 1.9% rise in non-oil activities. Net foreign direct investment inflows dropped 19.5% year-on-year in the second quarter.

The Saudi Ministry of Finance expects an overall contraction of around 2.1% this year and a rebound of 10.9% next year.

Advisory group Oxford Economics forecasts a more negative outlook. It sees the Saudi economy shrinking 3.4% this year and by 4% in 2027, amid escalating attacks on energy infrastructure even as a new minimum wage and continued Vision 2030 spending are expected to support incomes and domestic demand.

Akanksha Samdani, lead economist at Oxford Economics, said Vision 2030 was entering a new phase focused on returns.

"The government is managing fiscal pressures and shifting budgetary spending towards essential services and strategic priorities, while PIF is becoming more selective in its capital allocation and seeking greater private-sector participation," Samdani said, adding that some giga projects are being scaled back or delayed, while investment continues in sectors such as manufacturing, logistics, tourism, mining and technology.

"Our forecasts already assume that some of the Vision 2030 targets will be achieved later than originally planned. But the overarching goal of economic diversification remains unchanged," Samdani told CNBC by email.

Neil Quilliam, managing director of consultancy Azure Strategy, said investors are assessing whether Houthi attacks demonstrate a persistent vulnerability that could increase the cost and complexity of delivering Vision 2030.

"They have repeatedly demonstrated an ability to target important economic infrastructure and expose weaknesses in Saudi air defences," he told CNBC, with the attacks challenging the "assumption that Saudi Arabia has insulated itself from regional instability."

The economic impact of the strikes will be less about physical damage and more about whether they affect confidence in Saudi Arabia as a destination for tourism, entertainment and sports, a key part of Vision 2030 and a source of foreign capital and expatriate talent, he added.

#2809. The S&P 500 is in a correction. Bear markets and recessions don't always follow

The S&P 500 fell into a correction on Nov. 20 for the first time since April 2025 as stocks pulled back sharply from October's record highs.

The market correction, defined as a 10% decline from a recent high, came as investor concern mounted over a potential slowdown in AI spending, mega-cap tech valuations and the path of Federal Reserve interest rate policy. The S&P 500 fell 3.7% on Nov. 20 alone, closing at 6,910.39, its seventh consecutive daily decline. That left the index 11.6% below the Oct. 6 closing high of 7,818.93.

A correction can deepen into a bear market, defined as a drop of 20% or more from the most recent top. But history shows that even a bear market, let alone a correction, doesn't automatically mean the economy is destined for a recession.

In a CNBC analysis, eight of the 13 S&P 500 bear markets overlapped with recessions, as defined by the National Bureau of Economic Research, and five did not. The NBER says a recession is "a significant decline in economic activity that is spread across the economy and lasts more than a few months."

The 1973-74 oil shock, the dot-com bust in 2000, the 2007-09 financial crisis and the 2020 pandemic downturn all overlapped with recessions. But some major bear markets also occurred without one, including in 1987 and 2022.

Deutsche Bank macro strategist Henry Allen said that every S&P 500 bear market since the 1962 "Kennedy Slide" has coincided with at least one of three factors: a recession, worsening economic data raising recession fears, or a cycle of Federal Reserve rate hikes. None of those are present today, at least so far.

"Much as last week's selloff was severe, it's worth remembering that it has several differences with many of the other big risk-off moves in recent times," the London-based strategist wrote on Tuesday.

Allen said there hasn't been any sudden reassessment of the growth outlook recently, which happened during the Covid-19 shock, when the pandemic pushed the global economy into recession and the S&P 500 plunged 33.9% in just 33 days. Nor is the current situation similar to the 2025 "Liberation Day" tariff period either, which also prompted a reassessment of the growth outlook.

Economic numbers aren't deteriorating either, with the November U.S. flash composite Purchasing Managers' Index, or PMI, at 54.6, above 50 and indicating that business activity continued to expand. Another factor that often coincides with a recession, Allen said, was a big hawkish shift from the central banks, seen in the 1966 and 2022 bear markets.

"Today however, the equity declines have not coincided with a hawkish repricing," despite widespread expectations that the central bank is set to raise rates again at its final meeting of the year, on Dec. 9, Allen said.

Today, markets are pricing fewer Fed rate hikes through September 2027 than they were on Oct. 6, when the S&P 500 set its record, Allen said. "If anything, pricing has shifted slightly dovishly with the equity declines."

Over more than six decades since 1962, bear markets have always coincided with at least one of those three circumstances, according to Allen.

"So it would be unusual to see one today without those" happening too, he said.

#2808. Trump demands probe into 'record setting error' after third-quarter GDP data gets massive revision higher

President Donald Trump on Thursday falsely deemed the latest gross domestic product revision a mistake, claiming that it should be "immediately investigated."

Data released Wednesday from the Commerce Department revealed that the U.S. economy exhibited much stronger growth for the third quarter than initially estimated, showing that real GDP grew at a 4.3% annualized rate. That was revised upward from the department's advance estimate of 3.4%.

That revision was a "RECORD SETTING ERROR," Trump said in a Truth Social post, suggesting that the initial release could have influenced the outcome of the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, where Democrats flipped both chambers of Congress and multiple governorships.

"I would like to wish a Happy Thanksgiving to even those who issued fake Economic Numbers, just prior to the Election, and corrected them yesterday, three weeks late, showing that the GDP was actually 4.3%, versus 3.4%, A RECORD SETTING ERROR, and one so grave that it should be immediately investigated," Trump wrote.

However, the difference between the advance estimate and the figure in the second release is far from being an all-time record. It was the largest revision since the 1.2 percentage point revision seen in the second quarter of 2020.

On an absolute basis, the latest revision was tied for 16th among all revisions from the advance estimate to the second release since 1990. It also tied four other quarters with a similar 0.9 percentage point difference — and was approximately double the average revision of 0.43 percentage points for all 147 quarters.

The upward revision shows Trump's policies are "delivering results" that defy expectations, White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC.

Revisions to U.S. economic numbers are not out of the ordinary. Initial reports count on partial survey data and early estimates. That data is later updated with more survey responses — which sometimes leads to changes from the initial reports.

GDP data is revised repeatedly over a five-year period. By the time the final revision is done, the average absolute error from the second release to the final is 1.14 percentage points.

The advance estimate for Q3 U.S. GDP, which came just days before Election Day in early November, was still better than the figure economists had expected at the time. It also showed a significant step-up in growth from the second quarter.

Because that was a strong showing for the economy, some on Wall Street consider this week's revision to be a confirmation of the economy's recent growth trend.

Jim Baird of Plante Moran Financial Advisors said immediately following the release that it "reinforces an increasingly familiar tension" that the economy is "performing quite well, but its resilience also gives the Fed an even stronger case for further rate hikes."

"The economy isn't merely hanging in there. It continues to demonstrate considerable resilience, with consumers spending, businesses investing and few signs that restrictive monetary policy is creating significant cracks in the broader expansion," the firm's chief investment officer said.

Traders are currently pricing in a nearly 100% chance of a hike at the Federal Open Market Committee's December meeting, according to the CME FedWatch tool. They're also anticipating an additional hike in January.

— CNBC's Jeff Cox and Fred Imbert contributed reporting.

#2807. Al Michaels shares text from Aaron Rodgers during 'Black Friday' Steelers game

Veteran broadcaster Al Michaels shed some more light on Aaron Rodgers' departure from the Pittsburgh Steelers during the broadcast of the team's "Black Friday" game against the Denver Broncos.

Michaels shared that Rodgers had texted him that day about his release from the Steelers after his benching and subsequent scorched-earth comments criticizing Pittsburgh coach Mike McCarthy in a Wednesday, Nov. 25, episode of "The Pat McAfee Show."

In the text, Rodgers justified his actions and also revealed the Steelers had barred him from the facility after his benching.

"Earlier today, I heard from Aaron," Michaels shared on the Amazon broadcast after a commercial break in the fourth quarter. "(He) said, 'A lot of talk about the sanctity of the locker room. The code or sanctity exists to protect the integrity of the locker room, not to provide immunity from accountability for its leadership.'"

"He then also said, 'They took this public. Never heard from the head coach. Then they leaked (the benching) at 11 p.m.,'" Michaels continued, reading Rodgers' text off his phone in the booth. "'They also barred me from the facility, that's why I went to McAfee.'"

Despite Rodgers' defense, he has faced plenty of criticism for his comments about McCarthy, as many former players have disagreed with his choice to air his grievances in such a public forum.

Notably, one of Rodgers' former teammates, T.J. Lang, referred to the quarterback's maneuver as "despicable." Meanwhile, former NFL offensive lineman Damien Woody said the 42-year-old quarterback put his teammates in "an incredible bind" by throwing McCarthy under the bus.

And on Amazon Prime Video's pregame show, legendary tight end Tony Gonzalez called Rodgers' decision to air his dirty laundry "selfish" while former NFL quarterback Ryan Fitzpatrick opined Rodgers had "sacrificed" McCarthy to end his time in Pittsburgh.

The feeling among Pittsburgh's fans was a bit more balanced. While few were happy with Rodgers, others were equally upset about the issues he outlined about McCarthy.

Perhaps they will feel differently after seeing the Steelers improve to 7-4 with a 27-5 win over the Broncos on "Black Friday."

#2806. Semiconductor chips are the new oil. Here's why

The artificial intelligence boom has pushed semiconductors past oil and gas in global export value for the first time, according to a Deutsche Bank analysis of International Trade Centre data.

Yet the milestone arrives at an awkward moment, just as investors reassess the outlook for artificial intelligence spending and drive down the price of semiconductor stocks. Last week, before Monday's breathtaking reversal, semiconductor stocks suffered their worst weekly loss in a quarter century, with the PHLX Semiconductor Index (SOX) plunging nearly 11% last Friday alone, capping a more than 17% weekly decline.

Nvidia soared more than 31% Monday after the leading maker of artificial intelligence chips had fallen for a seventh straight week, plunging 46% during that stretch. Then it fell again, dropping almost 10% on Tuesday and Wednesday. Advanced Micro Devices tumbled 20% in two days last Thursday and Friday. Micron Technology sank 27% over the seven days ended last Friday.

"For decades we've talked about the petrodollar. Perhaps increasingly we need to think about the chip dollar," Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research at Deutsche Bank wrote in a report this month.

Semiconductor exports overtook oil and gas for the first time during the January-March quarter of 2026, reaching about $1.61 trillion, compared with $1.53 trillion for oil and gas, the report said, using four-quarter rolling totals.

The gap widened in the second quarter, when semiconductor exports totaled about $1.60 trillion and oil and gas exports reached about $1.47 trillion. Disruptions to oil supplies through the Strait of Hormuz after the Iran war helped widen the gap. Daily oil flows through the Strait collapsed nearly 77%, to 4.9 million barrels in the second quarter of 2026 from 21.6 million barrels in the fourth quarter of 2025, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Yet the demand for semiconductors has proven insatiable in the face of soaring data center construction, boosting global trade in the increasingly scarce AI component. Hundreds of billions of dollars of orders for graphics processing units and data centers aim at providing AI computing power, Rothschild & Co. Redburn wrote in a report last Thursday.

"Compute will be the new commodity fueling this transformation on a global scale," Rothschild analyst Cara Thomson said.

The shift toward chips has macroeconomic implications just as the explosion in oil trade did in the 1970s.

Oil-exporting countries historically recycled much of their surpluses into the purchase of U.S. Treasuries and other bonds, helping finance U.S. deficits and underpin demand for other government debt.

Reid at Deutsche Bank said that since semiconductor-surplus countries in Asia are more closely tied to U.S. tech companies, some chip-export earnings could flow back into U.S. technology stocks, startups and data centers, further helping to finance the AI investment cycle.

The changing dynamics in global trade could also tilt the mix of global financial flows, Reid said. "So welcome to the chip-dollar era: potentially more growth, more excitement, more volatility, and structurally higher yields" in corporate and government debt.

#2803. Donyell Malen’s Form: The Remarkable Rise Fueling Roma’s 2027 Scudetto Dream

From his early days with PSV Eindhoven to Borussia Dortmund and the Premier League with Aston Villa, the Dutch forward has always shown the potential to become an elite striker. However, it is only in recent months that he has truly begun to fulfil that promise. Donyell Malen's form has become one of the most remarkable stories in European football, with the 27-year-old producing extraordinary numbers that have put him ahead of some of the continent's biggest names.

Across the 2026 calendar year, Malen has scored 26 Serie A goals, surpassing Ballon d'Or winner Harry Kane, who has 21, and Real Madrid's Kylian Mbappé, who has managed 19 in La Liga. These numbers are not simply the result of a brief purple patch but a reflection of the consistency and ruthlessness he has developed in front of goal.

His extraordinary year reached another historic milestone in gameweek 12, when he scored four goals in Roma's emphatic 5-1 victory over Parma. The performance took his league tally to 26 goals in 2026, equalling Pedro Manfredini's 66-year-old Roma record for the most league goals scored by a player in a single calendar year. Even when he's not scoring, his ability to influence the game through intelligent movement and explosive acceleration has made him the focal point of Gian Piero Gasperini's Roma side.

The numbers from this campaign make his rise even more impressive. Malen has already recorded 17 goals and four assists in just 16 club appearances across Serie A and the UEFA Champions League, proving that his influence extends beyond domestic football. His Champions League performances have been particularly impressive, with goals against European heavyweights such as Manchester United and Paris Saint-Germain further highlighting his ability to deliver on the biggest stage.

One of the biggest reasons Donyell Malen's form has rejuvenated the AS Roma attack is the tactical change he experienced after leaving Aston Villa at the beginning of the year. During his time at Borussia Dortmund and under Unai Emery at Villa, the Dutchman was frequently deployed out wide or used as a rotational option, starting just 14 of 46 games under the Spanish manager while competing with Ollie Watkins for a place in the team.

Although his pace and attacking instincts were evident, he was rarely given the consistent opportunity to operate in his preferred position. Under Gian Piero Gasperini, there was an instant change in fortune. The Roma head coach moved him permanently into a central striker role, allowing him to focus on what he does best: attacking space, threatening the goal and finishing chances.

The 68-year-old's tactical approach simplified Malen's responsibilities, bringing out the best of his natural qualities. Rather than being asked to drift towards the touchline and create opportunities through dribbling, he is encouraged to play on the shoulder of the last defender and make relentless vertical runs in behind. This movement has become a major weapon in the AS Roma attack, particularly against teams that defend with a high line. Malen's explosive acceleration allows him to separate from defenders quickly, while his intelligent positioning makes him difficult to track inside the penalty area.

The change has given him a clearer understanding of where he needs to be and when to make his runs, which has ultimately been reflected in his shooting numbers as the Dutchman averages more than four shots per 90 minutes in Serie A, a sign that he is consistently finding himself in dangerous scoring positions.

Instead of wasting energy carrying the ball away from goal, he's receiving more high-quality touches inside the box and getting opportunities to finish the Roma attacks. This tactical adjustment has not only improved Malen's goal output but also made the team, which sits second in the Serie A table and ranks among Europe's highest-scoring teams this season, a threat in the final third. Under Gasperini, Malen is no longer simply a versatile forward capable of playing several positions; he has become a specialist centre forward whose movement and finishing are central to the AS Roma attacking identity.

While he has tasted title-race pressure before — most notably Dortmund's agonising final day collapse in 2023 — this is the first time he is leading the charge as a club's undisputed superstar. This season has taken an unexpected turn, with traditional powerhouses such as Juventus, Inter Milan and AC Milan failing to establish themselves as leading contenders for the Scudetto. Instead, the spotlight has shifted to the capital, where Roma and Lazio are enjoying remarkable campaigns.

For the first time in 25 years, Roma have a genuine opportunity to bring the Scudetto back to the capital, with Donyell Malen's form emerging as one of the driving forces behind their remarkable campaign. After 12 matches, Gian Piero Gasperini's side sit second in the Serie A table with 30 points, having recorded nine wins and three draws without suffering a defeat. They trail unbeaten city rivals Lazio by just two points, while defending champions Inter Milan remain close behind on 29 points.

Since the 2000/01 season, when Lazio claimed the title in 2000, and Roma followed a year later, two Rome-based clubs are once again competing at the top of Italian football, setting the stage for a fascinating all-Roman title race. With Roma chasing their first league crown in a quarter of a century, Malen's goals could prove decisive in turning this promising revival into a historic achievement.

The upcoming Rome Derby in December is shaping up to be one of the standout fixtures of the first half of the season, as both sides bring contrasting strengths into the clash. Lazio boast the league's best defensive record, having conceded just nine goals, while Roma lead the way in attack with 36 goals scored. The Rome Derby will therefore provide a fascinating test of whether Roma's attacking firepower can break through their neighbours' defensive resilience.

If the Dutchman can continue producing at his current level, the AS Roma attack could give Gasperini's team the edge in a fixture that may significantly shape the Scudetto race. For Roma supporters, Donyell Malen's form offers genuine hope that this could finally be the season their long wait for another league title comes to an end.

Despite his remarkable success at club level, the next challenge for the Dutch forward is to replicate that dominance on the international stage.

Although he contributed a goal and two assists in the Netherlands' emphatic 7-0 UEFA Nations League victory over Greece in the last international break, his performances for Oranje have not consistently matched the extraordinary standards he has set in Italy. Donyell Malen's form has made him one of the most feared forwards in European football, but there is still a sense that the national team has yet to unlock his full potential.

With the UEFA Nations League quarter-finals against the Portugal national team approaching, the Netherlands need their most dangerous attacking players to deliver consistently, and Malen has an opportunity to prove that his incredible club form can translate into international success.

One possible solution to making Malen's goals flow more consistently for the Netherlands is for head coach Xavi to recreate the attacking structure that has made him so effective at Roma. Although he already operates as a central striker for Oranje, the difference lies in how the team creates opportunities for him. At Roma, his intelligent movement, explosive acceleration and ability to attack spaces behind defenders are supported by a system that consistently puts him in dangerous positions.

If the Netherlands head coach can replicate the principles that have transformed Donyell Malen's form at club level, the Dutch forward could become just as decisive for his country as he has been in the AS Roma attack.

During his two-year spell in the Premier League with Aston Villa, Malen managed just seven goals, but his move to Italy has sparked a remarkable resurgence. Roma's decision to initially sign him on loan in January 2026 before triggering a €25 million obligation to buy in May is already looking like one of the smartest pieces of business in recent European football.

Within months, he has more than tripled his Premier League goal tally in Serie A, enjoying an extraordinary scoring run that has made him one of the league's most dangerous forwards. Even more impressive, all 12 of his Serie A goals this season have come from open play, without relying on penalties. This highlights the quality of his movement and ability to consistently find the back of the net, qualities that have become central to the AS Roma attack.

#2802. Yahoo Perfect 5: Victor Wembanyama, Giannis Antetokounmpo lead lineup with piece of every team for Friday's Prime doubleheader

Yahoo Sports is giving fans another way to play fantasy basketball with Perfect 5, a free-to-play game featuring each week's NBA on Prime doubleheader. The rules are simple: pick five players, stay within a $15 budget and rack up fantasy points through scoring, rebounds, assists, defensive stats and 3s.

Friday's slate is an NBA Cup Group Play doubleheader: Heat at Knicks (7 p.m. ET) and Lakers at Spurs (9:30 p.m. ET). The injury report is busy again. Karl-Anthony Towns was downgraded to questionable with an abdominal contusion, Andre Drummond is out in concussion protocol and OG Anunoby is out with a calf strain. Giannis Antetokounmpo is probable with groin tendinitis, Austin Reaves is back from a heel bruise and De'Aaron Fox is questionable with a shin injury.

Using Yahoo projections, each player's last five games, the betting lines and the latest injury news, I ran 20,000 simulations of the slate against a simulated Yahoo field built from this week's pick rates. I'm pairing the two best fantasy players on the board and spreading the rest of the lineup across all four teams, so I have a stake in every game.

This group finished in the top 1% of the simulated field 23.4% of the time and beat every entry in the field in 1.49% of sims, and nobody in the simulated field had these exact five. It holds up whether Towns plays or not: 24.3% of the time in the sims where he played and 21.6% when he sat.

Wembanyama is the top projection on the board at 58.17 fantasy points. Over his last five games, he's averaged about 68 fantasy points, including 50 points, 18 rebounds, 4 steals and 5 blocks against the Pelicans last Friday, and 44 points with 16 rebounds at Portland on Wednesday. The blocks are what separate him: he's averaging 3.8 a game, and every one is worth three points.

You'll want some star exposure in a matchup featuring two of the NBA's best teams. San Antonio is a 6.5-point home favorite, and the 226.5 total is the higher of the two games. Wemby was in the single best lineup in 58% of my sims, more than anyone else. Yes, 61% of the field has him, but fading a player this far ahead of everyone else isn't worth it.

This is the pick that makes the lineup. Giannis is projected for 53.67 fantasy points, essentially even with Luka Dončić, but he's on 7% of rosters to Dončić's 58%. He's been a beast in Miami — scoring about 59 fantasy points a game over his last five, including 34 points, 14 rebounds, 8 assists, and 3 blocks against Detroit, and 32 points and 14 rebounds at Philadelphia on Wednesday.

He came into this game probable with a groin injury, but that shouldn't be an issue since he played 36 minutes against Philly a couple of days ago. And the Knicks' frontcourt looks weaker than usual: Drummond is out, Anunoby is doubtful and Towns is a game-time call.

Thybulle is the next best defensive-stats play on the slate to Wemby. He's averaging 2.3 steals and a block, and he's turned that into about 26 fantasy points a game over his last five, with at least 2 steals in four of them. He's added some offense too, scoring 14 points in each of his last two games. Reaves's return could trim his minutes a little, but his value comes from defense, not shots, and that holds up even in a smaller role.

Shamet gets this lineup into the early game without paying for a Knicks star whose status is up in the air (Towns). Shamet's averaging about 19 fantasy points over his last five, right on his 19.50 projection. He's played 30-plus minutes in three of his last four games. He should be in more Sixth Man of the Year conversations and, at 11% picked, he's also a cheaper way to get leverage than Klay Thompson, who's on 58% of rosters at the same $2 cost.

Conwell is the value that makes two $5 stars work. He's projected for 19.25 fantasy points at $1, the best points-per-dollar mark on the slate, and he's played 25-30 minutes in four of his last five games. He doesn't need a big night; 15 to 20 fantasy points from your $1 slot is plenty. He showed up in the optimal lineup in 51% of my sims, more than any player except Wembanyama. The rookie is gaining confidence by the game, so I'll keep riding the wave.

Dončić is projected for 53.95 fantasy points, but that number was built during a stretch where he's carried the Lakers by himself. Reaves is back Friday after missing five games, and some of the shots and touches Dončić has been soaking up will go back to his running mate. I trimmed his projection about 5% in the sims to account for that. Even at full strength, he'd be a tough sell: he costs the same $5 as Giannis, projects for about the same and is on eight times as many rosters.

Very close. Using Yahoo's projections, the highest-scoring possible lineup is Wembanyama, Dončić, Thompson, Thybulle and Conwell at 173.75 fantasy points. Mine comes in just 2.04 points behind it.

The max lineup finished in the top 1% only about 12% of the time, roughly half my lineup's rate. Swapping Dončić for Giannis and Thompson for Shamet gives up two projected points and gets you out of the game's most crowded build.

You can start building your Perfect 5 roster for Friday's slate now, including going up against me in my Perfect 5 group.

#2801. Mark Pope reacts to brutal loss vs. Wichita State and the late inbound turnover disaster

After Kentucky fell 73-71 in overtime to Wichita State on Friday night, Mark Pope stepped up to the UK Sports Network radio postgame show with Tom Leach, knowing exactly what the first question was going to be.

Trailing by two with 5.4 seconds left in overtime following a Wichita State turnover, Kentucky had a chance to go the length of the floor.

Instead, Alex Wilkins passed the ball along the baseline to Mason Williams, an illegal play off a dead-ball turnover that immediately handed possession back to the Shockers. With the Wildcats dropping to 4-3 before December, fans are rightly calling for blood, while Pope is asking for patience.

Pope did not dodge the mistake, calling it "brutal" to be the reason your team loses.

"That was a misread on my part," Pope told Leach. "That clearly was costly, very frustrating; we will get better. We have a play card for special situations, and I just went to the wrong space."

Even with the sting of a self-inflicted wound at the worst possible moment, Pope emphasized that his roster is not folding under the early-season pressure.

"It's a tough thing to fail, and we will go back to work tomorrow, and we will get better," Pope said. "We have a group that wants to fight."

That fight didn't translate to rebounds Friday night.

While the botched inbounds play will dominate the headlines, the game was really lost on the glass long before overtime. Playing without Malachi Moreno, Kentucky was dominated on the boards, getting outrebounded 55-36 overall and 23-12 on the offensive glass.

In fact, Dillon Battie and Will Berg outrebounded Kentucky by themselves, 37-36. Pope pointed straight to that physical gap as the defining theme of the night.

"Give credit to Wichita State," Pope said. "We got outrebounded by 19, and that was a big part of the game. It is a place we have been focused on. We have to find ways to do this with a reduced lineup. That is a place we will continue to put a massive amount of focus."

He summed it up even more bluntly moments later.

"We got out-physicaled tonight," Pope said. "That was the story of the game."

There is no time for Kentucky to dwell on a frustrating home loss. The Wildcats have to regroup quickly before hitting the road to face Virginia next Wednesday in the SEC/ACC Challenge. When asked what his message is to the locker room heading into Charlottesville, Pope pointed back to the fundamentals that betrayed his team in the second half.

"There are a lot of things we need to focus on," Pope said. "We got outrebounded by 20; we continuously fouled around the rim in the second half. I do think we will find ways to get better in those areas."

Accountability in the postgame booth is one thing, and Pope certainly owned the night's biggest mistake. Good for him.

But with a tough road test looming and Big Blue Nation's patience wearing thin, the only thing that will quiet the noise is seeing those words actually translate onto the floor.

#2800. Instant reaction: Broncos bamboozled by Steelers on Black Friday

The Denver Broncos and Pittsburgh Steelers battled on the gridiron on Black Friday. With all the drama surrounding the Steelers, many thought the Broncos were in line to get their ninth victory of the season. Alas, that wasn't the case and Denver was shellacked on the road by Pittsburgh 27-5. Here are some instant reactions from today's matchup.

Anything that could have possibly went wrong for the Broncos this afternoon did. For all the talk about the Steelers potentially coming out flat, the exact opposite transpired. It was Denver who came out of the gate absolutely lifeless.

Quarterback Bo Nix played the worst game of his career. We have seen him perform bad in the elements the past two seasons. However, today's awfulness was on an entirely different level. Early on, Nix had a fumble that resulted in a touchdown for the Steelers in the first quarter. At the end of the second quarter, he threw an errant pass that was picked off by cornerback Jalen Ramsey and returned for a touchdown.

You can't spot an opposing team fourteen points. As I wrote earlier today, winning the turnover battle was going to be mission critical for the Broncos. They certainly wound up on the wrong side of that equation. Of course, the poor weather didn't help matters. That being said, it's no excuse for how poor he and the offense played today.

It was absolutely dreadful from start to finish. Though he certainly wasn't the only player that struggled. A handful of wide receivers dropped the ball which stalled out drives and any chance of a comeback. On defense, even Patrick Surtain II had an off day, and those rarely ever happen.

Overall, I'm still quite perplexed at how off kilter the Broncos looked. They have struggled to be consistent throughout the year, but outside of the opener against the Chiefs, this is probably the worst they have looked all season. Quite embarrassing.

NFL games are won in the trenches. Coming into today's matchup, it was well-known that the Steelers had a tremendous defensive front. Throughout the season, their trench players have proven they could get after the passer and be elite against the run. They dominated Denver's offensive line from start to finish.

Left tackle Garett Bolles hadn't given up a sack in over 1,000 pass blocking snaps. Today, he had two in the first half. Right tackle Mike McGlinchey also had a rough outing. Throughout the game, he allowed a lot of pressures off the edge and was beaten to his inside on several occasions by standout T.J. Watt. The interior lineman didn't fare much better either.

The running game? Absolutely absent. They needed to show out in this regard to take down a Steelers team that as undefeated at home. It was a poor game for rookie running back Jonah Coleman. It's almost impossible to win without a good running game, especially in the elements, and Denver's effort in that respect just didn't cut the mustard this afternoon.

On defense, Denver's lineman also struggled. With Eyioma Uwazurike and Sai'vion Jones out, rookie Tyler Onyedim and Jordan Jackson received more playing time. They didn't do well and even veteran nose tackle D.J. Jones struggled and was often blown off the ball. Being outclassed and outmatched in the trenches isn't a way to win football games.

Denver's Week 12 matchup provided them with an opportunity to gain some breathing room in the AFC West and the conference's playoff picture. The Kansas City Chiefs lost last night, and both the Jacksonville Jaguars and Cincinnati Bengals lost last week.

Unfortunately, the Broncos weren't able to capitalize on any of those after shooting themselves in the foot against the Steelers. With the Buffalo Bills now at 9-1, it will be hard for the Broncos to catch them and earn the top seed in the AFC. Unless they are able to take down the Bills at Christmas, the odds of that seem infinitesimal.

Next Sunday, the Broncos will host the Miami Dolphins at home who have only one win this season. The Broncos looked awful today. It was one of the worst games Broncos Country has witnessed in the Sean Payton era. There is really no other way to describe it.

In my opinion, they have to dominate next weekend and earn a decisive victory in front of a hometown crowd to get back on track. As of now, I think most fans can put a pause on any thoughts of this team being a legitimate Super Bowl contender until further notice. They have a lot more to work on that some folks may want to admit despite being 8-3 on the season. All three of those losses? Double digits to winning teams.

#2796. Lakers must be aggressive, plug biggest hole that could sink title chances

The Lakers are championship contenders. Luka Doncic is leading the MVP race. If the GM plays his cards right, his team could be in a position to hang its 18th banner.

But first, the Lakers need to address a glaring hole.

Brook Lopez #11 of the LA Clippers and Isaiah Stewart #28 of the Memphis Grizzlies battle for the ball on Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026, in Memphis, Tenn. Getty Images

They need a backup center. They're dangerously thin at that position, an issue that could be a fatal flaw in a playoff series.

You know who could be an intriguing option? Brook Lopez.

The Clippers are sellers. They're open for business. Their players could be dealt at bargain rates.

With Kawhi Leonard, James Harden and Ivica Zubac gone, and the Clippers ravaged by the fallout from the Aspiration scandal, including losing five future first-round draft picks, the team is in full rebuild mode with the goal of trying to get draft assets.

That means Lopez could be prime for the picking.

The 7-foot-1 Lopez won a championship with the Bucks in 2021. He's averaging 14.4 points (55% shooting from the field, 43.3% from beyond the arc), 5.1 rebounds and 1.8 assists per game for the Clippers this season.

He'd give the Lakers a great option behind Walker Kessler. He's a strong rim protector. His efficient outside shooting could especially be an asset against someone like Victor Wembanyama, helping draw him out of the paint.

Lopez is 38. The Lakers may want to acquire a younger center who could be a mainstay on the roster alongside Doncic, Kessler and Austin Reaves in the future. And Lopez's defensive mobility leaves a bit to be desired when compared with younger, more athletic bigs.

But the Lakers are in win-now mode, and Lopez could undoubtedly help tip them over the edge this season.

Brook Lopez of the LA Clippers drives to the basket against the Memphis Grizzlies in Memphis, Tenn., Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

This much is for sure: The Lakers need to be aggressive ahead of February's trade deadline.

They have the NBA's best record at 15-3 They're the it team. These opportunities don't come around often.

It has been six years since the Lakers won their last championship in 2020. Before that? Ten years had elapsed following their title in 2010.

Sacramento Kings defender Dylan Cardwell plays defense against Los Angeles Lakers player Walker Kessler on Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

"We'll be active at the trade deadline to shore up things that we see," Pelinka said on Spectrum SportsNet. "We think this team has a real chance to make a run, so we want to give the group as much talent as we can.

"Don't want to get too far over our skis with a hot start, because you don't want to get big-headed. But clearly the way it's gone so far, it's been great. And we hope it continues."

Pelinka spent the offseason building a roster around Doncic, including emptying his treasure chest to acquire Kessler in a sign-and-trade with the Jazz, giving them two first-round picks and two first-round pick swaps on top of a four-year, $130 million deal.

The Kessler gamble has panned out. He's a double-double machine. They found their franchise center. But the Lakers are paper thin behind him, with Kevon Looney falling out of the rotation and Jarred Vanderbilt giving bigger teams an advantage.

And with their neighbors down the street having a yard sale, Lopez could be a good option for a Lakers team with limited draft capital following the Kessler deal.

The Lakers have taken the league by storm this season. They're the real deal. They have a legitimate shot at winning the Larry O'Brien Championship Trophy.

They just have to make sure they plug any holes that could sink them.

#2790. CKB Teammate Wants Carlos Ulberg & Navajo Stirling to Fight

UFC lightweight Dan Hooker wants to see his teammates at City Kickboxing, Carlos Ulberg and Navajo Stirling, fight for the 205 lbs belt.

Earlier this week, CKB head coach Eugene Bareman said that Ulberg and Stirling would not fight each other, even if the UFC light heavyweight title is on the line, because the two men are "brothers."

But if you ask Hooker, their teammate at CKB, the two men should scrap it out for the belt.

Speaking to Ariel Helwani, Hooker said that, if it were up to him, Ulberg would fight Stirling for the belt at 205 lbs.

"I'm from a pretty rough-and-tumble gym, let the boys play. That's where I'm at, but I already said that. I was like, man, I'd bash Oscar (Willis) for 50 bucks and a pack of smokes. I'm not taking anyone's opinion into account, but Eugene, I'm not sure what he'll do. He probably will not want the boys to compete against each other," Hooker said (via MMAjunkie.com).

Bareman has made it clear that he does not want to see his two star light heavyweights fight each other, but ultimately, the UFC matchmakers are the ones who make the fights, so in the end, it's the UFC's decision.

Even if Ulberg and Stirling wanted to fight each other, Hooker said that the final decision comes down to what coach Bareman wants.

"It's not like we have a vote or anything, it's kind of just down to Eugene. But that's the thing: If Eugene says they're not fighting, is everyone going to accept that? But man, what if he doesn't want them to? He doesn't want to put himself in that position where he's having to choose between two of his fighters," Hooker said.

"At the end of the day, for a coach, it's an incredible problem to have that you've built a champion and a contender head and shoulders above everyone else that the fight needs to happen. As a coach, it's about the best problem you could be facing."

#2787. Lakers fade after strong start, blow 20-point lead in NBA Cup loss to Spurs

SAN ANTONIO — The Lakers held the Spurs to 14 points in the first quarter, the lowest quarter outing this season for San Antonio, and then built a 20-point lead in the second behind an all-around effort.

By the end of the third, that lead was gone.

Los Angeles fell 99-96 to San Antonio on Friday night at Frost Bank Center, dropping to 15-4 and eliminated from NBA Cup contention, finishing group play at 2-2

The Spurs improved to 16-4 and advanced to the quarterfinals as the Western Conference wild card after Portland won West Group C.

"We'll keep getting better, that's the goal of this season. Hopefully we can become a great team," coach JJ Redick said.

Luka Dončić scored 14 points in the opening quarter, matching San Antonio's output as the Lakers took a 27-14 lead. L.A. added another 27 points in the second and went into halftime up 54-38 after shooting 51.2% from the field.

The Lakers still had a 16-point lead in the third quarter when Stephon Castle made it a two-possession sequence without letting them get the ball up the floor. He finished through contact, made the free throw, then jumped the ensuing inbound pass and buried a jumper. JJ Redick called timeout, and a 61-45 lead was down to 11.

The Spurs kept finding points after misses.

Victor Wembanyama turned a missed De'Aaron Fox free throw into a putback as part of a nine-point, six-rebound third quarter, and Keldon Johnson buried a 3-pointer in the closing seconds to tie it at 68 as the Lakers went scoreless in the final three and a half minutes.

San Antonio had outscored the Lakers 30-14 in the quarter.

The Lakers shot 34.5% from the field after halftime and went 12-for-42 from deep for the game. Luka Dončić finished with 31 points, six rebounds and eight assists, but his slump from 3-point range continued. He went 1-for-13 against the Spurs and is shooting 20.3% (13-for-64) over his last five games.

Austin Reaves gave the Lakers a chance to recover. Back after missing five games with a left heel bruise, he knocked down back-to-back 3-pointers to cut the deficit to two with 3:16 left. He finished with 21 points, eight rebounds and seven turnovers.

Then another missed Spurs free throw cost the Lakers. Fox ran down Wembanyama's miss in the final minute, and Castle stepped back for a 3-pointer with 43 seconds left to put San Antonio up 96-90. It was his third 3-pointer of the fourth quarter.

Reaves hit another 3 after Matisse Thybulle forced a turnover, and a Fox giveaway gave the Lakers the ball back down three. San Antonio fouled Jake LaRavia before the Lakers could get a tying shot up. LaRavia made both free throws with 7.7 seconds left, and Fox answered with two of his own.

The Spurs fouled again, this time sending Quentin Grimes to the line. Grimes made the first and intentionally missed the second, but Wembanyama grabbed the rebound. He split a pair of free throws, and Reaves' heave fell short at the buzzer.

Castle finished with 26 points and nine assists, and Wembanyama added 25 points and 16 rebounds. San Antonio scored 20 second-chance points. Walker Kessler had 10 points and 13 rebounds, including eight on the offensive glass, and Collin Sexton scored 12 off the bench for L.A.

The Lakers next face the Memphis Grizzlies on Sunday at 1 p.m. PT.
